However, I recall this specific formulation: the proof of noninterference via logical relations where terms are related only when they are "similar" up to erasure. Key: two terms t1, t2 of type T with security of T's indirect reader Low... Hmm.

Let me think about what the theorem says. Given t1, t2 both have type T_secret in empty context. Context C is typed with hole T_secret -> T_result. ground T_result, transparent T_result, and not (flows_to (indirect_reader of T_secret) (indirect_reader of T_result)). Then plug C t1 and plug C t2 have same result upon evaluation.

Note: same_result only says if both evaluate, erase results equal. This is termination-insensitive. Note also that the theorem is a "noninterference" statement: the secret (t1, t2 with type T_secret) is not observable in the result if the secret indirect reader cannot flow to result indirect reader.

Wait, but the theorem doesn't require t1 and t2 to be closed... Actually has_type empty t1 T_secret implies open terms with... Hmm, has_type empty allows free variables? Locally nameless: has_type doesn't enforce locally closedness; open body (tm_fvar x) is typed. But bvars could appear and never be opened; then evaluation could get stuck. But the statement is termination-insensitive: if both evaluate to values, results equal. Stuck terms don't evaluate to values, so vacuous.

Hmm, this is the standard "termination-insensitive noninterference" statement.

So relation: define logical relation `R (T : ty) (kappa : security)`? Let's think about the standard "logical relation for noninterference" (e.g., from "A Theory of Secure Information Flow" / "Probabilistic Relational Hoare Logic"?). Actually the classic: `t1 ~_T t2` if for all observer level... Let's define:

Wait but we need the relation to be usable for open terms and computations. The theorem needs to relate plug C t1 and plug C t2 where C is an arbitrary typed context. Standard approach: prove "fundamental theorem" that typing implies the logical relation at appropriate level, where the relation is defined on terms (not just values), and is closed under the context rule. Then use that plug C t1 and plug C t2 are related at level Low; then show related-at-T_result-at-level-Low implies same_result.

The relation must be defined so that:
- For public types (all security ≤ Low observer), the relation relates terms that have same erased behavior.
- For secret types with indirect reader High, the relation is more permissive (any two terms).

The key idea: relation at level `l` (observer level) for type T: t1 ~ t2 iff ... they're "indistinguishable to attacker at level l". For a type with reader level High > l, the observer can't read the value, so any two values are related (as long as both are values / both diverge, i.e., "termination-insensitive"). For a type with reader Low ≤ l, the observer can read the value, so they must be equal after erasure, or related componentwise.

But the indirect reader matters for control flow: e.g., if a High computation branches on a secret and produces public outputs, then High branch values must be... Hmm, the "protection" via indirect_reader is exactly about this.

Definitions:
- `flows_to l1 l2` means information at level l1 may flow to l2. High flows only to High.
- `security := { reader; indirect_reader }`.
- `security_le k1 k2` iff reader1 ≤ reader2 and indirect1 ≤ indirect2.
- `ty_protect l T` raises both labels of T's security to join with l.
- Typing for app: if t1 : Arrow T1 T2 kappa and t2 : T1, and flows_to kappa.reader r, then app : ty_protect kappa.indirect_reader T2. So the result's security labels are raised to the join of T2's security and kappa.indirect_reader. Similarly case, fst, snd, if.

The dynamic semantics: `tm_app (abs T body k) v r --> tm_protect k.indirect_reader (open body v)` — so at runtime the result gets protected at the indirect_reader level of the function's arrow security. And `tm_protect l v --> protect_value v l` which raises the value's security label by join with l (only for values whose top-level constructor carries a security annotation; for functions the arrow's security, etc.).

And `tm_if (inl v k) yes no r --> tm_protect k.indirect_reader yes`, similarly for inr. So the branch result is protected at the condition's indirect reader.

The key type-soundness property: if `v : T` and `flows_to (security_of T).reader l`... hmm.

Let's think about what `transparent T` means: `transparent_at (security_of T) T`, which requires `security_le (security_of T) (security_of T)` (trivial) and recursively all subtypes have security ≤ security_of T, i.e., each component's reader and indirect reader are ≤ the top's reader/indirect reader. Combined with `T_result`'s indirect reader Low (since theorem premise), all components are Low/Low. Also `ground` means no arrows, so result values are units, pairs, inl/inr of ground values — fully "first-order".

OK let's now design the logical relation carefully. Let me think about what's needed for each type former.

Actually, the cleanest standard approach: define the relation on *values* (Vrel) and define the term relation as "if t1 evaluates to v1 then t2 evaluates to some v2 with Vrel v1 v2, and vice versa", i.e.:

Now the standard logical relation for such a calculus with protect... Let me think about the relation directly.

I'll define for each type T and observer level l:

Semantics idea: an observer at level l. Arrow: Vrel l (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) v1 v2 :=

Hmm, but the arrow's behavior isn't just its direct content. The indirect reader of the function's arrow security κ.indirect represents the level of the *effect* of applying the function: the dynamic semantics protects the application result at κ.indirect. I think the correct relation is: at level l,
- Vrel l (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) v1 v2 := if flows_to kappa.reader l then (forall u1 u2, Trel l T1 u1 u2 -> Trel l (ty_protect kappa.indirect T2)? (app v1 u1 ...) ...) hmm.

Alternatively, we can define the arrow value relation to directly require: for related arguments u1,u2 (at T1, level l), the applications `tm_app v1 u1 r` and `tm_app v2 u2 r` are Trel at T2 but ... with what protection? Hmm, the dynamic semantics automatically protects at kappa.indirect. But if the relation is defined on arbitrary r, maybe just fix one r? Since the typing rule requires flows_to kappa.reader r. Note the term annotation r is not semantically relevant except it's checked flows_to kappa.reader r, so any r that satisfies is equivalent (r ∈ {High} if kappa.reader=High, or r ∈ {Low,High} if kappa.reader=Low). The dynamic step uses kappa.indirect regardless of r. So the relation could quantify over r with flows_to kappa.reader r.

Alternatively, define the relation via "Trel l T2 (app v1 u1 r) (app v2 u2 r)" using Trel at the *unprotected* T2? But the runtime protection means the values would be protected at kappa.indirect, so they'd be related at ty_protect kappa.indirect T2, which might be a weaker requirement than at T2 (secret typing is weaker). Using unprotected T2 is stronger and might not be provable from typing. So use ty_protect kappa.indirect T2.

Let me think about what's needed for compatibility of T_App in the fundamental theorem: t = app t1 t2 r, type ty_protect kappa.indirect T2. Let's think about a "CIU"-style or "contextual" relation. Actually, here's a clean idea: define the term relation *extensionally* in terms of evaluation:

Trel l T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> Vrel l T v1 v2.

Then:
- Fundamental theorem for a variable: if env related, subst gives t1, t2 related: that's given by env condition.
- For t = app t1 t2 r: assume evaluates (subst app) v1 and v2. We need Vrel at result type. Evaluations give: app a1 b1 r -->* v1. By determinism/decomposition, there exist va1, vb1 with a1 -->* va1, b1 -->* vb1, value va1, value vb1, and app va1 vb1 r -->* v1. Since va1 is a value of arrow type (from typing), it's an abs; then step: app (abs T body k) vb1 r --> tm_protect k.indirect (open body vb1) -->* v1, so evaluates (tm_protect k.indirect (open body vb1)) v1. From this, we need Vrel at ty_protect k.indirect T2 of v1 and v2. Hmm, we have relatedness of a1 ~ a2 at arrow type, but the arrow value relation defines behavior on related args; we know vb1 related to vb2? We get that from Trel T1 b1 b2 applied to evaluations of b1 to vb1 and b2 to vb2, giving Vrel T1 vb1 vb2. Then arrow relation gives Trel (ty_protect ...) (app va1 vb1 r) (app va2 vb2 r)? But we have va1 (value), and we need to know a2 evaluates to va2 and vb2 evaluation... From Trel at T1 (b1 b2) we get Vrel T1 vb1 vb2. From a1 ~ a2 at arrow type via evaluations a1 -->* va1: Trel at arrow type is forall values... but Trel gives Vrel l (Arrow...) va1 va2 where va2 is a value reached by a2. Then Vrel arrow gives (for related args vb1 vb2) Trel (ty_protect k.indirect T2) (app va1 vb1 r) (app va2 vb2 r) for any r with flows_to kappa.reader r — good, r satisfies.

Then since tm_protect k.indirect (open body1 vb1) evaluates to v1 (from the decomposition), and app va1 vb1 r --> tm_protect..., we need Trel's definition to be closed under single steps / multi-steps: Trel (ty_protect...) (app va1 vb1 r) (app va2 vb2 r) is defined as "for all evaluations to values w1, w2, Vrel w1 w2". We know app va1 vb1 r --> tm_protect ... -->* v1 and value v1, so evaluates gives Vrel v1 (whatever v2 evaluates to). Actually careful: Trel gives forall w1 w2, evaluates (app va1 vb1 r) w1 -> evaluates (app va2 vb2 r) w2 -> Vrel w1 w2. We have evaluates (app va1 vb1 r) v1 because app va1 vb1 r --> tm_protect ... -->* v1 and value v1. Good. Similarly for the other side. So Vrel v1 v2. 

So the term relation via evaluations works fine, as long as we can decompose evaluations of compound terms. This requires "evaluation decomposition" lemmas for each construct, which we can prove by induction on the multi-step derivation, using inversion of step. Because the step relation has all the structure, decomposition is straightforward but maybe many cases (for each construct: app, pair, fst, snd, inl, inr, case, protect, if). Let me estimate: about 9 decomposition lemmas. Each by induction on multi with step inversion. Doable.

The key question remains the arrow case. Let's think semantically. For an arrow type T = Arrow T1 T2 κ, what should relatedness at observer level l mean?

If κ.reader ≤ l (observer can see the function's security at the reader level), then: for all related arguments u1, u2 at T1 (at level l? or at some level related to κ?), the applications are related at ... what? But also, should the *argument* relation be at level l? The function is called with arguments; the function's body might send secrets to the argument? Hmm, functions are pure; the argument value is just passed. For noninterference, arguments that are indistinguishable at level l should yield results indistinguishable at level l. But the reason κ.indirect matters: the function application's result may depend on the function's internal secrets? Hmm, actually the protection at κ.indirect is because the *function value itself* may have been created under a secret context (indirect level), e.g., an abs created inside a High branch. Then applying it at Low gives a result which depends on that secret decision, so the result must be protected to High.

So the arrow's κ.indirect represents "the level of secrets that influenced this function value". Applying it produces a result influenced at that level. So the application result is protected at κ.indirect.

Now the relation at level l should say: two function values are indistinguishable to an observer at level l. This means: the observer can call them with arguments they can construct... but they can call with any arguments, including secret ones? In the logical relation setting, we quantify over all pairs of related arguments at level l. If the argument type T1 is secret, then any two args related (at level l) — and then the result must be related at level l, which requires the function's result to be protected at level l... Hmm.

Simplification: Since l = Low always, define:

value_rel (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ => 
      if flows_to κ.reader Low then
        (match v1, v2 with
         | tm_inl a1 _, tm_inl a2 _ => value_rel T1 a1 a2
         | tm_inr a1 _, tm_inr a2 _ => value_rel T2 a1 a2
         | _, _ => False
         end)
      else True
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      if flows_to κ.reader Low then
        (match v1, v2 with
         | tm_pair a1 b1 _, tm_pair a2 b2 _ => value_rel T1 a1 a2 /\ value_rel T2 b1 b2
         | _, _ => False
         end)
      else True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      if flows_to κ.reader Low then
        forall u1 u2, value_rel T1 u1 u2 -> term_rel T2 (tm_app v1 u1 Low) (tm_app v2 u2 Low)
      else True
  end

with term_rel T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> value_rel T v1 v2.

Let me reconsider the relation definition to handle annotation mismatches more gracefully. Key insight: maybe define the relation not in terms of the type's annotations alone, but with the semantics "v1 and v2 are related at type T and observer l" defined as: for all security levels j "compatible"... Alternative approach used in some developments: two-level relation, where the relation at type T is defined by "T's security, when protected at the appropriate level, makes the values indistinguishable". Let me think about what properties we need:

1. Fundamental theorem: typing ⊢ relation.
2. Relation at public ground types implies erase equality.
3. Relation is compatible with subtyping (raising security weakens).
4. Runtime protection at l maps relation at T to relation at ty_protect l T.

Alternatively, let's define the value relation by "the value's observable behavior at level l is the same", where we interpret each value recursively:
- For a unit value: nothing to observe.
- For a pair: observe components if the pair's security allows reading at l.
- For inl/inr: observe tag and payload if allowed.
- For abs: observe behavior of applications if the function's security allows.

Now, the fundamental theorem. Let me write the relation definitions concretely. To avoid mutual recursion issues in Coq, define them as a single Fixpoint on the type with a "fuel"? Types are inductive, and term_rel calls value_rel on subcomponents; value_rel's arrow case calls term_rel on T2 (a subcomponent). The term relation itself doesn't recurse on the type structure except via value_rel. Since value_rel is defined by recursion on T, and its arrow case mentions term_rel at T2 (structurally smaller than Arrow T1 T2 κ), we can define them mutually:

Fixpoint value_rel (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ => True? or erase equality?
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ => ...
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ => ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ => ... term_rel T2 ...
  end
with term_rel (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> value_rel T v1 v2.

In Coq, mutual Fixpoints require the recursive calls to be on structurally smaller arguments. In value_rel's arrow case, term_rel T2 — T2 is a subterm of T, OK. But term_rel's body calls value_rel T — same argument T. In mutual fixpoints, calling the other function on the same argument is allowed? For mutual fixpoints, the guard condition requires recursive calls on structurally smaller arguments *with respect to the whole mutual block*? Actually Coq's guard checker for mutual fixpoints allows calls to another function in the block on the same argument if... Hmm, I believe Coq requires that recursive calls (including to mutually defined functions) be on structurally smaller arguments, except that calls to another function of the block on *equal* arguments might be allowed? Let me recall: For nested/mutual recursion, the guard condition allows `term_rel T` from value_rel T if T is the same? I think in mutual fixpoints, the recursion must decrease on at least one argument when calling the other function... Hmm, actually I recall Coq allows mutual fixpoints where f calls g on the same argument? No—I think it's not allowed; the guard condition requires the recursive argument to be a subterm of the current function's argument, for any call to the mutual block. Wait, but standard STLC logical relation developments do exactly this:

Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop := ...
with T (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop := forall v, eval t v -> V T v.

Hmm, term calls V on same T: I believe this IS allowed in Coq's mutual fixpoint guard checker? Let me think about Coq's guard condition: In a Fixpoint with multiple definitions (Fixpoint ... with ...), a recursive call to any function in the block must be on a structurally smaller argument than the corresponding argument of the *current* function. Specifically, the "recursive argument" is determined per function; when f calls g, g's decreasing argument must be a subterm of f's decreasing argument. Calling g on the same argument T: is T a subterm of T? The guard checker uses strict structural decrease; `T` itself is not a strict subterm. Hmm, but there's a special case: for a call from f to g, the decreasing argument can be the same if? I don't think so.

The term relation can be defined *after* value_rel by well-founded? No, it's mutual.

Another standard trick: define value_rel T v1 v2 by recursion on T, where the arrow case quantifies over arguments and then uses the *evaluation relation* with value_rel at T2 — but we can inline: term_rel T2 t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> value_rel T2 v1 v2. Inlining into the arrow case gives a formula with value_rel T2 nested, which is a recursive call on subterm T2 — fine! And the top-level term_rel can be defined after value_rel as a plain Definition (not Fixpoint) that refers to value_rel. So:

Fixpoint value_rel (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ => value v1 /\ value v2
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (if flows_to κ.reader Low then
         ((exists a1 a2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 k2 /\ value_rel T1 a1 a2) \/
          (exists a1 a2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inr a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 k2 /\ value_rel T2 a1 a2))
       else True)
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (if flows_to κ.reader Low then
         (exists a1 a2 b1 b2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\ value_rel T1 a1 a2 /\ value_rel T2 b1 b2)
       else True)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (if flows_to κ.reader Low then
         (forall (u1 u2 r : tm? no r is label),
            value_rel T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ.reader r ->
            forall w1 w2, evaluates (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 -> evaluates (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 ->
            value_rel (ty_protect κ.indirect_reader T2) w1 w2)
       else True)
  end.

Here the arrow case inlines the term relation at ty_protect κ.ind T2, calling value_rel on (ty_protect κ.ind T2). Is ty_protect κ.ind T2 a structural subterm of (Arrow T1 T2 κ)? In Coq's guard checker, the recursive call must be on a subterm of the original argument T. ty_protect κ.ind T2 is a *function application*, not a subterm! Coq will reject this as not structurally decreasing. Hmm.

Workaround: don't use ty_protect in the relation; instead, apply value_rel at T2? But the runtime protection makes the values more protected. But we established: at observer Low, if the type T2's relation is at a *more protected* variant, it's weaker. Hmm, we need the right type.

Option (b): define `value_rel` as a Fixpoint on a natural number n = size of T, taking T as an argument, with a check that size T < n? Hmm, typical: 

Fixpoint value_rel_aux (n : nat) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct n} : Prop :=
  match n with
  | 0 => True
  | S n' => match T with ... value_rel_aux n' (ty_protect ... T2) ... end
  end.

But ty_protect preserves the size, so we need n' ≥ size (ty_protect κ.ind T2) = size T2 < size T ≤ n. So we'd call with n' = n - 1, and need size T2 < n'? Option (f): Use Coq's `Inductive`-style logical relation? Some developments define the logical relation as an Inductive with rules for each type constructor; then recursion is not an issue (it's a positive inductive). But then the relation is the *least* relation rather than a fixpoint; for our proofs (we need to derive consequences), inductive definition means we'd need inversion and the clauses are implications in the "forward" direction... Hmm, an inductive definition like:

Inductive VR : ty -> tm -> tm -> Prop :=
| VR_Unit : forall κ v1 v2, value v1 -> value v2 -> VR (Ty_Unit κ) v1 v2
| VR_SumL : forall T1 T2 κ a1 a2 k1 k2, flows_to? no, the gate is a premise condition based on κ... 
   Note the gate depends on κ, but as a constructor premise we can write: forall T1 T2 κ a1 a2 k1 k2, flows_to κ.reader Low -> VR T1 a1 a2 -> VR (Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ) (tm_inl a1 k1) (tm_inl a2 k2)
| VR_SumL_secret : forall T1 T2 κ v1 v2, ~ flows_to κ.reader Low -> value v1 -> value v2 -> VR (Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ) v1 v2
| ...

This is the typical "inductive logical relation" and it's fine, but the arrow constructor would need to mention the term relation with applications: 

| VR_Arrow : forall T1 T2 κ v1 v2, value v1 -> value v2 -> flows_to κ.reader Low ->
    (forall u1 u2 r, VR T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ.reader r -> forall w1 w2, evaluates (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 -> evaluates (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 -> VR (ty_protect κ.ind T2) w1 w2) ->
    VR (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ) v1 v2

This refers to VR at (ty_protect κ.ind T2) — but as an inductive definition, positivity requires VR to occur strictly positively; in the arrow constructor, VR occurs in the argument of a forall in a premise position (negative position!), which would violate strict positivity. Actually `(forall u1 u2 ..., VR T1 u1 u2 -> ... -> VR T2' w1 w2) -> VR (Arrow ...) v1 v2`: VR occurs negatively in the domain of an arrow. Positivity checker would reject. Hmm, unless we use a different style: mutual inductive? Same issue.

So inductive is problematic for the arrow (higher-order).

Option (h): Define the relation using fuel *and* prove that the relation doesn't depend on fuel once fuel ≥ size, via a lemma. This is standard in some developments ("fuel-based logical relations"). Let's think: define

Fixpoint VR (n : nat) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match n with
  | 0 => True
  | S n' =>
    match T with
    | Ty_Unit κ => value v1 /\ value v2
    | ...
    | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ => value v1 /\ value v2 /\ (gate -> forall u1 u2 r, VR n' T1 u1 u2 -> ... -> forall w1 w2, evaluates (app v1 u1 r) w1 -> ... -> VR n' (ty_protect κ.ind T2) w1 w2)
    end
  end.

All recursive calls use n', strictly smaller, so guard passes regardless of the type argument. Then define value_rel T v1 v2 := VR (size T + 1) T v1 v2? or VR (size T) T... And prove lemmas by induction on n with the type size bounded: e.g., 

Lemma VR_mono : forall n n' T v1 v2, n ≤ n' -> VR n T v1 v2 -> VR n' T v1 v2? Is that true? VR with more fuel might demand more (the arrow quantifies with n' fuel on components). Hmm: VR n T with larger n is *stronger* (more recursive unfolding). So monotonicity would be the wrong direction. We need: the relation stabilizes for n ≥ size T. Prove: forall n, size T ≤ n -> (VR n T v1 v2 <-> VR (size T) T v1 v2)? Hmm, or prove the lemmas directly by induction on n with side conditions.

Since all our types are finite and typed terms' types are wf, and we call VR at ty_protect ... T2 which has the same size as T2, the fuel needed is bounded by size. We can set the convention: value_rel T := VR (size T) T? Let's be careful about what fuel is needed: At type T with fuel n, the arrow case uses fuel n' for the components and for ty_protect κ.ind T2. So we need n' ≥ size (ty_protect κ.ind T2) = size T2. If n = size T = 1 + size T1 + size T2, then n' = n - 1 ≥ size T2? Since size T1 ≥ 1, n - 1 = size T1 + size T2 ≥ size T2 + 1 > size T2. ✓. For the ground cases, components get n' which is ≥ their sizes. So by induction, if we define VR with fuel = size T, all recursive calls have enough fuel. But the relation at smaller fuel may still be defined; we need the proofs to go through when we call VR n' T1 with n' possibly > size T1 (more fuel than needed). So we should prove a "fuel irrelevance" lemma: VR n T == VR n' T for n, n' ≥ size T. Or define VR in a way that saturates: at each call use exactly size? Can't, since it's a Fixpoint on n.

Since we only deal with observer Low and wf types, define:

VR T v1 v2 :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ => value v1 /\ value v2
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (if flows_to κ.reader Low then
         ((exists a1 a2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 k2 /\ VR T1 a1 a2) \/
          (exists a1 a2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inr a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 k2 /\ VR T2 a1 a2))
       else True)
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (if flows_to κ.reader Low then
         (exists a1 a2 b1 b2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\ VR T1 a1 a2 /\ VR T2 b1 b2)
       else True)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (if flows_to κ.reader Low then
         (forall u1 u2 r, VR T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ.reader r ->
            if flows_to κ.indirect_reader Low then
              (forall w1 w2, evaluates (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 -> evaluates (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 -> VR T2 w1 w2)
            else True)
       else True)
  end.

- Take evaluations. Decompose: val1 evaluates to abs1 (value) with annotation κv1; arg1 evaluates to u1. Vrel (Arrow T1 T2 (High,Low)) abs1 abs2 (from Trel and evaluations). Now, what does Vrel at this arrow type say? Gate flows_to κ.reader Low = flows_to High Low = False ⇒ my current definition gives True. So no info. Then the applications step (flows_to κv1.reader r=High ✓): app abs1 u1 High --> tm_protect κv1.ind (open body1 u1). We need Vrel T2 of the final values. Without info from the arrow relation, we can't proceed.

So the arrow relation must be stronger: it must capture behavior even when κ.reader = High, as long as κ.ind = Low (behavior observable). What if κ.ind = High? Then results are protected at High; opaque; trivial. So maybe the gate for the arrow should be on κ.ind (whether the function's behavior is secret-influenced), not κ.reader! Let's hypothesize:

Vrel (Arrow T1 T2 κ) v1 v2 := value v1 /\ value v2 /\
  (if flows_to κ.indirect_reader Low then
     forall u1 u2 r, Vrel T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ.reader r -> (applications related at T2... )
   else True)

Hmm, but what level should the applications be related at, and under what protection? Let's think semantically: if κ.ind = Low, the function's behavior is not secret-influenced, so two related functions should produce related results when applied to related arguments — at the type T2? But the runtime protection at κv.ind (the runtime annotation of the function value). Hmm, and κ.ind = Low but the runtime annotation κv ≤ κ (term annotation ≤ type annotation), so κv.ind ≤ Low ⇒ κv.ind = Low ⇒ runtime protection is a no-op. So the result values are the body's values, related at T2 if the bodies are related. ✓. And the static result type ty_protect κ.ind T2 = ty_protect Low T2 = T2. Consistent!

But wait, is it sound to require behavior for a (High, Low) function even though the observer can't apply it at Low (they'd need r = Low, but flows_to High Low fails)? The observer could still apply at High (r = High) and get a public T2 result — so yes, the behavior is observable at Low, so the relation must constrain it. ✓.

What about a (Low, High) function (not wf)? κ.ind = High ⇒ results protected at High, behavior unobservable; relation trivial. But κ.reader = Low means it can be applied at any r, but the results are protected. ✓.

What about (Low, Low)? Both: behavior observable, applies anywhere. Relation: behavioral on related args, results at T2. ✓.
(High, High): applies only at High, results protected at High ⇒ unobservable at Low. Relation trivial. ✓.

So the gate should be on κ.indirect_reader, and the result relation at T2 (since κ.ind = Low ⇒ protect is identity). Interesting! Let's double check with wf: wf means ind ≤ reader. So cases: (Low,Low), (High,Low), (High,High). (Low,High) is non-wf and excluded. So:
- (Low,Low): behavioral, applicable anywhere.
- (High,Low): behavioral, applicable at r = High only.
- (High,High): trivial.

Hmm, so for the arrow relation, the reader level doesn't matter except in the applicability condition (which is used in the T_App fundamental theorem: the typing gives flows_to κ.reader r, and we need it to apply the arrow relation's clause? If the clause quantifies over r with flows_to κ.reader r, the typing supplies exactly that. ✓).

Now let's redo the T_App case with the new arrow relation:
- κ.ind = High: goal term_rel (ty_protect High T2) apps → trivial (gate closed at top). ✓
- κ.ind = Low: goal term_rel T2 apps (since ty_protect Low T2 = T2). Given evaluations, decompose to abs1, u1 etc. Vrel Arrow abs1 abs2 gives (if ind Low) forall related args, any r with flows_to κ.reader r: applications related at T2. We have Vrel T1 u1 u2 from the argument's Trel. And flows_to κ.reader r from typing ✓. So Trel T2 (app abs1 u1 r) (app abs2 u2 r), and evaluations of app... to w1, w2 give Vrel T2 w1 w2 ✓. 

But hold on: the values abs1, abs2 come from evaluating val1, val2: Trel Arrow val1 val2 gives Vrel Arrow abs1 abs2. In the fundamental theorem's T_Abs case, we must *prove* Vrel Arrow for the abs values; there κ = the term's annotation. Evaluations of app abs_i u_i r: since abs_i are values? Hmm, in the T_Abs case, are the abs values? Yes, we proved Vrel must be for values; but in proving the arrow clause we need abs1, abs2 to be values to step. In the T_Abs fundamental theorem case, we have value abs1, value abs2 (locally closed) — from evaluations. ✓. And u1, u2 values (from Vrel T1) ✓. So ST_AppAbs applies. Then multi through tm_protect Low: evaluations of app abs_i u_i r correspond to evaluations of the body (via protection decomposition at Low). Then use term_rel T2 of bodies. ✓.

So with the gate on κ.ind, the T_Abs case works. 

Now let's re-examine the ground types. For sums: should the gate be on reader or ind? Value observability: the observer can case on a sum value if flows_to κ.reader r for their r; they're at Low, so they need κ.reader = Low... but they could also case at High and get ty_protect κ.ind T. If κ.ind = Low, the result is public → they can observe the tag! So a sum with reader High, ind Low: caseable at r = High, result public. So the tag is observable at Low. Hence the gate should be on κ.ind for sums too!

Hmm, wait. But can the observer "choose" r = High? The label r is part of the term, chosen by the program author, and the typing checks flows_to κ.reader r. If κ.reader = High, the program can only case at High; the result is ty_protect κ.ind T. If κ.ind = Low, that's T, public. So yes, the tag is observable. So the gate should be ind for sums and prods too.

What about unit? Unit has no tag; nothing to observe.

So general rule: the *indirect_reader* determines observability of content? Let's think about what reader means then. For sums/prods, reader seems to be about "what contexts can eliminate this value" (r must be ≥ reader), while ind is about "the level of the result of elimination". So observability of the tag = whether the elimination result is readable = ind ≤ Low. Yes! So gate on ind.

Hmm, but wait: for the *content* (components) of a pair: to observe the components, you eliminate with fst at r; the result type is ty_protect κ.ind T1 = protect at ind. If ind = Low, protection is identity, so component T1's own security governs. So the pair's gate being open means components are related per their own types. ✓.

So I now propose: Vrel at ground types gates on `flows_to κ.indirect_reader Low` (ind = Low). And the value relation for sum:
VR (Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ) v1 v2 := value v1 /\ value v2 /\
  (if flows_to κ.ind Low then (same side + component VR) else True).

But hold on: What about the *reader* level? Is it completely irrelevant to the value relation? The value is readable (reader Low) but the elimination result is protected at High. So the tag isn't observable at Low. ✓.

Hmm, but is it sound to have the value relation ignore the reader entirely? The reader matters for whether the *program* may eliminate at a Low label. If the program eliminates a (High, Low) sum at r = High, the result is unprotected and public. So the tag is observed. If the program eliminates a (Low, High) sum at r = Low (allowed since flows_to Low Low ✓), the result is protected at High → unobservable. So indeed, reader doesn't affect observability. Nice.

Wait, but for arrows: the reader determines whether the observer can apply the function at *their own level* r = Low. And when ind = High, the result of any application is protected at High, so unobservable. But hmm, what about the function's *divergence*? Termination-insensitive, ignore.

But there's another aspect for arrows: if κ.reader = High, the observer cannot even "have" the function at Low? But functions are values; they can be passed around, stored in pairs, etc. The type system allows using a secret function only in High contexts (applications at High). But the value relation at Low must account for behavior as argued.

So maybe the general principle: the gate is `flows_to (security_of T).indirect_reader Low`, i.e., ind = Low.

Hmm, but wait. Let's check the typing rule T_App again: result type ty_protect κ.ind T2. If the arrow relation gates on ind and the result is related at T2 when ind = Low, consistent. T_If: result ty_protect k.ind T where k is the condition's security ✓.

So everything is consistent with gating on ind! And this makes the "protect" mechanism coherent: protecting at l raises both reader and ind; the ind part determines observability; raising ind to High makes it opaque.

Now let's re-examine the final theorem's derivation of "all gates open". transparent T_result gives security_le (security_of T_i) (security_of T_result) for each node, in particular flows_to (security_of T_i).ind (security_of T_result).ind. With T_result.ind = Low, we get (security_of T_i).ind = Low ✓. Great, so all gates open. The theorem's premise uses indirect readers exactly because gates are on ind. Nice—the premise directly gives closed gate at T_secret. So I'm now fairly convinced: gate on indirect_reader. Let me redo the design:

VR (T) (v1 v2) :=
  value v1 /\ value v2 /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ => (if flows_to κ.ind Low then (exists k1 k2, v1 = tm_unit k1 /\ v2 = tm_unit k2) else True)
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ => 
      if flows_to κ.ind Low then
        ((v1 = inl a1 k1 /\ v2 = inl a2 k2 /\ VR T1 a1 a2) \/ (inr...))
      else True
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      if flows_to κ.ind Low then (v1 = pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = pair a2 b2 k2 /\ VR T1 a1 a2 /\ VR T2 b1 b2) else True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      if flows_to κ.ind Low then
        (forall u1 u2 r, VR T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ.reader r ->
           forall w1 w2, evaluates (app v1 u1 r) w1 -> evaluates (app v2 u2 r) w2 -> VR T2 w1 w2)
      else True
  end.

Hmm, in the arrow clause, should the applications be related at T2 or ty_protect κ.ind T2? Since we're in the case ind = Low, ty_protect Low T2 = T2. ✓ So T2.

But wait, there's still the issue of runtime annotation κv vs type annotation κ. Need: for related args at U1, r with flows_to κ'.reader r: applications related at U2. We have Vrel U1 u1 u2 -> Vrel T1 u1 u2 (contravariance via subtype U1 T1 — monotonicity in the other direction). Wait, S_Arrow: subtype (Arrow T1 T2 κ) (Arrow U1 U2 κ') requires subtype U1 T1 and subtype T2 U2. That's monotonicity along subtype: Vrel U1 -> subtype U1 T1 -> Vrel T1. ✓ (direction: from subtype X Y, Vrel X -> Vrel Y). So with X = U1, Y = T1 ✓.

Then the κ-clause applies: need flows_to κ.reader r. We have flows_to κ'.reader r and security_le κ κ' ⇒ flows_to κ.reader κ'.reader and flows_to κ'.reader r... does that give flows_to κ.reader r? flows_to is transitive-ish: flows_to a b and flows_to b c -> flows_to a c. Then applications related at T2 (from the source clause). Need related at U2: use subtype T2 U2 monotonicity ✓.

Wait, but the source clause requires Vrel T1 u1 u2 which we derived, and gives term relation at T2 via evaluations — precisely: Vrel Arrow clause says forall u1 u2 r, VR T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ.reader r -> forall w1 w2, evaluates... -> VR T2 w1 w2. That's exactly Trel T2 (app v1 u1 r) (app v2 u2 r) unfolded. ✓.

Also need the gate: if κ'.ind = Low, since security_le κ κ' gives flows_to κ.ind κ'.ind = flows_to κ.ind Low ⇒ κ.ind = Low ✓ (flows_to X Low iff X = Low).

And the value-ness requirement is included in both. ✓.

So monotonicity in the gate-on-ind formulation works.

Now, what about the *protection lemma*: VR T v1 v2 -> VR (ty_protect l T) (protect_value v1 l) (protect_value v2 l). With gate on ind: ty_protect l T has top ind = join (security_of T).ind l. Gate at Low: flows_to (join ind l) Low ⇔ join ind l = Low ⇔ ind = Low ∧ l = Low. If l = Low, protect_security κ Low = κ and protect_value v Low = v, so the statement reduces to VR T v1 v2 ✓. Now, the annotation bound in VR: let me define runtime_security carefully. Since VR is only applied to values, define:

Definition value_security (v : tm) : security :=
  match v with
  | tm_unit κ => κ
  | tm_abs _ _ κ => κ
  | tm_pair _ _ κ => κ
  | tm_inl _ κ => κ
  | tm_inr _ κ => κ
  | _ => public  (* arbitrary; shouldn't happen for values *)
  end.

And VR T v1 v2 := value v1 /\ value v2 /\ security_le (value_security v1) (security_of T) /\ security_le (value_security v2) (security_of T) /\ body.

Now, the decomposition lemmas. We need for each construct an evaluation decomposition. Let me define a general lemma about multi and step inversion. First, note step is deterministic-ish but we don't need determinism. We need:

Lemma values_no_step : forall v t, value v -> ~ (v --> t).
Proof by cases on value and inversion of step. Each step rule's LHS has a specific constructor; can value be of that constructor? tm_unit is a value; is there a step with tm_unit on LHS? No. tm_abs: no. tm_pair: no. tm_inl/tm_inr: no. So by induction on step derivation / cases. Actually, step's LHS constructors are: tm_app, tm_pair, tm_fst, tm_snd, tm_inl, tm_inr, tm_case, tm_protect, tm_if. Values are tm_unit, tm_abs, tm_pair, tm_inl, tm_inr. Intersection: tm_pair, tm_inl, tm_inr! But the step rules with tm_pair LHS: ST_Pair1 (t1 --> t1') and ST_Pair2 (value v1, t2 --> t2'). If tm_pair v1 v2 is a value, then value v1 and value v2 (v_pair), so ST_Pair1 requires v1 --> which contradicts values_no_step for v1 (mutual induction on value/step?). Hmm, we need a mutual induction. We can prove value_no_step by induction on the value derivation: for v_pair, given step (tm_pair v1 v2 κ) t, invert: ST_Pair1 with v1 --> t1', contradict IH (value v1); ST_Pair2 with value v1 (✓) and v2 --> t2', contradict IH (value v2). Similarly v_inl: step (tm_inl v κ) t via ST_Inl: v --> t', contradict IH. ✓. So induction on value derivation works.

Also need: multi value inversion: multi v u -> value v -> u = v. By induction on multi: refl ✓; step v u' then multi u' u: but value v and step v u' contradicts value_no_step. ✓.

Decomposition lemmas (for multi from a compound term to a value):
1. app: multi (tm_app a b r) w -> value w -> exists T body k vb, multi a (tm_abs T body k) /\ value (tm_abs T body k) /\ multi b vb /\ value vb /\ flows_to k.reader r /\ multi (tm_protect k.ind (open body vb)) w.
   Proof by induction on multi (app a b r) w:
   - refl: value (app a b r) impossible (inversion: app is not a value).
   - step (app a b r) u, multi u w: invert step:
     * ST_AppAbs: a = tm_abs T body k, b = v, value (abs) ✓ value v ✓ flows ✓, u = tm_protect k.ind (open body v). Then multi u w ✓. Take vb = b. multi a a ✓ (refl), multi b b ✓.
     * ST_App1: a --> a'. u = app a' b r. IH on multi u w gives T body k vb with multi a' (abs) etc. Then multi a (abs) by prepending step ✓.
     * ST_App2: value a, b --> b'. IH gives multi b' vb; prepend ✓.
2. pair: multi (tm_pair a b k) w -> value w -> exists va vb, multi a va /\ value va /\ multi b vb /\ value vb /\ w = tm_pair va vb k. Proof: induction; refl: value (pair a b k) ⇒ a, b values and w = the pair ✓. step: ST_Pair1/2 with IH.
3. fst: multi (tm_fst t r) w -> value w -> exists v1 v2 k, multi t (tm_pair v1 v2 k) /\ value v1 /\ value v2 /\ flows_to k.reader r /\ multi (tm_protect k.ind v1) w.
4. snd: similar.
5. inl: multi (tm_inl t k) w -> value w -> exists v, multi t v /\ value v /\ w = tm_inl v k.
6. inr similar.
7. case: multi (tm_case t b1 b2 r) w -> value w -> exists (v k) and either multi t (tm_inl v k) /\ ... /\ multi (tm_protect k.ind (open b1 v)) w, or the inr version.
8. protect: multi (tm_protect l t) w -> value w -> exists v, multi t v /\ value v /\ w = protect_value v l.
9. if: multi (tm_if c y n r) w -> value w -> exists v k, (multi c (tm_inl v k) /\ value v /\ flows_to k.reader r /\ multi (tm_protect k.ind y) w) \/ (multi c (tm_inr v k) /\ ...).
10. abs: multi (tm_abs T body k) w -> value w -> w = tm_abs T body k. Similarly maybe for values in general we have multi_value_inv. Use that.

These are all provable by induction on multi + step inversion.

Now the fundamental theorem's cases need these decompositions; the "same-side" property in case/if: we use IH's VR at the sum type to know both sides have the same tag. In the case, side 1 might be inl and side 2 inl (from VR) ✓.

In Coq, we case on the side of each decomposition; for mismatched sides, the VR hypothesis is contradictory (it says both inl or both inr). So we can derive the needed structure.

Let me now write the full VR:

Fixpoint value_rel (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  value v1 /\ value v2 /\
  security_le (value_security v1) (security_of T) /\
  security_le (value_security v2) (security_of T) /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ =>
      (if flows_to κ.(indirect_reader) Low
       then exists k1 k2, v1 = tm_unit k1 /\ v2 = tm_unit k2
       else True)
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      (if flows_to κ.(indirect_reader) Low
       then (exists a1 a2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 k2 /\ value_rel T1 a1 a2)
            \/ (exists a1 a2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inr a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 k2 /\ value_rel T2 a1 a2)
       else True)
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      (if flows_to κ.(indirect_reader) Low
       then exists a1 a2 b1 b2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\ value_rel T1 a1 a2 /\ value_rel T2 b1 b2
       else True)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      (if flows_to κ.(indirect_reader) Low
       then forall u1 u2 r, value_rel T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ.(reader) r ->
              forall w1 w2, evaluates (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 -> evaluates (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 ->
              value_rel T2 w1 w2
       else True)
  end.

The recursive calls: value_rel T1, T2 with T1, T2 subterms of T ✓ (guard OK). Note the arrow case calls value_rel T2 in the body of a forall — structurally smaller ✓.

term_rel as a Definition after value_rel:
Definition term_rel (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> value_rel T v1 v2.

OK so we need has_type_lc. Let's plan the lemmas:

A. Label/security basic lemmas:
- flows_to_refl: forall l, flows_to l l.
- flows_to_trans: forall l1 l2 l3, flows_to l1 l2 -> flows_to l2 l3 -> flows_to l1 l3.
- flows_to_low: forall l, flows_to l Low <-> l = Low. (by destruct l)
- not_flows_to_high_low: ~ flows_to High Low.
- security_le_refl, security_le_trans.
- protect_security_low: forall κ, protect_security κ Low = κ.
- protect_security_high: forall κ, protect_security κ High = {| reader := High; indirect_reader := High |}? Hmm: protect_security κ High: reader = join κ.reader High = High (by cases); ind = High. So equals secret (public? secret := {reader High; ind High}).
- protect_security_mono: security_le κ1 κ2 -> security_le (protect_security κ1 l) (protect_security κ2 l).
- ty_protect_low: forall T, ty_protect Low T = T.
- security_of_ty_protect: security_of (ty_protect l T) = protect_security (security_of T) l. By destruct T ✓.
- protect_value_security: for values v, value_security (protect_value v l) = protect_security (value_security v) l. By cases on v.
- protect_value_low: forall v, value v -> protect_value v Low = v. By cases on v, destruct security.
- protect_value_is_value: value v -> value (protect_value v l).
- security_of_arrow etc. simpl.

`transparent T := transparent_at (security_of T) T`, where transparent_at κ T requires security_le (security_of T') κ for all subnodes T'. The theorem gives transparent T_result. Actually, one more consideration: the induction on has_type. Since has_type has an index (context, tm, ty), the induction principle generated is `has_type_ind` with the motive over Γ t T. We want to prove `forall Γ t T, has_type Γ t T -> P Γ t T` where P Γ t T := forall ρ1 ρ2, env_rel Γ ρ1 ρ2 -> term_rel T (subst_env ρ1 t) (subst_env ρ2 t). Standard: `intros Γ t T H; induction H; ...`. The IH for T_Abs will be about the premise with x universally quantified. However, there's a subtlety: in the T_Abs case, the premise is `(forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Γ x T1) (open body (fvar x)) T2)`. The induction principle gives as IH a function: `forall x Hx, P (update Γ x T1) (open body (fvar x)) T2 (H0 x Hx)`. Since P is not a function of the derivation, the IH would be `forall x (Hx : ~ In x L), forall ρ1 ρ2, env_rel ... -> term_rel ...` ✓ (ihas_type_ind? Actually Coq's generated induction principle for such premises gives IH as `forall x, ~ In x L -> P ... (H x Hx)`, but since P ignores the derivation, we get the desired statement). We may need to use `induction H` and Coq will handle it; but sometimes for nested binders it's easier to use `remember` or the `induction` with `generalizing`.

Potential issue: the IH will be stated with the derivation argument; since P doesn't depend on the derivation, Coq might still give IH with the specific derivation term. Should be fine.

Also, the T_Sub case: IH: P Γ t T (derivation). Goal P Γ t U. Fine.

Let me also think about how to handle the `if` in the Fixpoint: 

Fixpoint value_rel (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  value v1 /\ value v2 /\
  flows_to (value_security v1).(indirect_reader) (security_of T).(indirect_reader) /\
  flows_to (value_security v2).(indirect_reader) (security_of T).(indirect_reader) /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ => if is_low κ.(indirect_reader) then (exists k1 k2, v1 = tm_unit k1 /\ v2 = tm_unit k2) else True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ => if is_low κ.(indirect_reader) then
        ((exists a1 a2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 k2 /\ value_rel T1 a1 a2) \/
         (exists a1 a2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inr a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 k2 /\ value_rel T2 a1 a2))
      else True
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ => if is_low κ.(indirect_reader) then
        (exists a1 a2 b1 b2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\ value_rel T1 a1 a2 /\ value_rel T2 b1 b2)
      else True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ => if is_low κ.(indirect_reader) then
        (forall (u1 u2 : tm) (r : label), value_rel T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ.(reader) r ->
           forall w1 w2, evaluates (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 -> evaluates (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 -> value_rel T2 w1 w2)
      else True
  end.

The recursive calls: value_rel T1, T2 — structurally smaller ✓. In the arrow case, value_rel T2 appears inside the forall — the guard checker should accept since T2 is a subterm of (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ). ✓

Now the guard: Fixpoint value_rel (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T}. Calls value_rel T1 and value_rel T2 where T1/T2 are subterms of T ✓.

Hmm, S_Trans requires transitivity of the relation: subtype T U -> subtype U V -> relation at T implies at U implies at V. With induction on the subtype derivation (T U), the S_Trans case has IH for the two premises. But the induction principle for subtype: `subtype_ind`. The statement: forall T U, subtype T U -> forall v1 v2, value_rel T v1 v2 -> value_rel U v1 v2. Induction on the derivation: 
- S_Unit: subtype (Unit κ1) (Unit κ2); need VR (Unit κ2) from VR (Unit κ1) with security_le κ1 κ2. Unfold both; bounds: from flows_to κv.ind κ1.ind and flows_to κ1.ind κ2.ind ⇒ ✓; gate: if κ2.ind = Low then κ1.ind = Low (since flows_to κ1.ind Low) ⇒ source gate open ⇒ both tm_unit ✓.
- S_Sum: subtype (Sum T1 T2 κ1) (Sum U1 U2 κ2) with subtype T1 U1, subtype T2 U2, security_le κ1 κ2. IH: VR T1 -> VR U1, VR T2 -> VR U2. Need VR (Sum U1 U2 κ2) from VR (Sum T1 T2 κ1). If gate at κ2 open (κ2.ind = Low) then κ1.ind = Low (security_le) ⇒ source structural: same side with VR T1/T2 → IH gives VR U1/U2 ✓. Bounds: trans ✓. If gate closed: need value-ness and bounds only ✓.
- S_Prod similar.
- S_Arrow: subtype (Arrow T1 T2 κ1) (Arrow U1 U2 κ2) with subtype U1 T1, subtype T2 U2, security_le κ1 κ2. Note contravariance U1 T1. IHs: (forall v1 v2, VR U1 v1 v2 -> VR T1 v1 v2) and (VR T2 -> VR U2). Need: VR (Arrow T1 T2 κ1) -> VR (Arrow U1 U2 κ2). If gate at κ2 open: source gate open (κ1.ind = Low). Behavior: forall u1 u2 r, VR U1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ2.reader r -> ... VR U2 of results. Use IH-contra to get VR T1 u1 u2; get flows_to κ1.reader r from security_le + flows_to κ2.reader r; apply source clause to get VR T2 results; then IH-cov to get VR U2 ✓. Bounds: value_security of v1 ≤ κ1 ≤ κ2 ✓ trans. Gate closed: bounds only, with target ind High ⇒ flows_to _ High ✓.
- S_Trans: IH1, IH2: compose ✓.

So induction on the subtype derivation with the statement including v1 v2. Lemma lc_at_S : forall k t, lc_at k t -> lc_at (S k) t.
Proof: induction t; simpl; intros; try easy; 
- bvar: simpl; lia (i < k -> i < S k).
- abs: apply IHt; assumption.
- app/pair: destruct H; split; auto.
- case: destruct as [H0 [H1 H2]]; repeat split; auto.

Lemma lc_open_rec_fvar : forall body x k,
  ~ In x (fv body) -> (lc_at k (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) body) <-> lc_at (S k) body).
Proof: induction body; intros x k Hx; simpl; try (split; intros; auto; fail)...
Cases:
- tm_bvar i: simpl. open_rec k (fvar x) (bvar i) = if k =? i then fvar x else bvar i. lc_at k of that: if i =? k then True else i < k. RHS: i < S k.   In Coq, `if Nat.eqb k i` vs `Nat.eqb i k`: open_rec uses `Nat.eqb k i`. So the condition is k = i. Fine.

Lemma has_type_lc : forall Γ t T, has_type Γ t T -> locally_closed t.
Proof: induction 1 (induction on the derivation).
- T_Var: lc_at 0 (fvar x) = True ✓.
- T_Unit: ✓.

- T_App: lc_at 0 (app t1 t2 r) = lc_at 0 t1 /\ lc_at 0 t2; IH ✓.
- T_Pair: ✓.
- T_Fst/T_Snd: ✓.
- T_Inl/T_Inr: ✓.
- T_Case: lc_at 0 (case t b1 b2 r) = lc_at 0 t /\ lc_at 1 b1 /\ lc_at 1 b2. IH for t; for bodies: IH gives forall x ∉ L, lc_at 0 (open body1 (fvar x)); pick x fresh L ++ fv b1; lc_open gives lc_at 1 b1 ✓.

- T_Sub: IH ✓.

Definition is_low (l : label) : bool := match l with Low => true | High => false end.
Lemma is_low_true : forall l, is_low l = true <-> l = Low. destruct l; simpl; intuition discriminate.
Lemma is_low_false : forall l, is_low l = false <-> l = High. destruct l; simpl; intuition discriminate.
Lemma is_low_flows : forall κ, is_low κ.(indirect_reader) = true <-> flows_to κ.(indirect_reader) Low. destruct κ as [r i]; destruct i; simpl; intuition.

Now the Fixpoint. Note Coq's `{struct T}`.

Definition:

Fixpoint value_rel (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  value v1 /\ value v2 /\
  flows_to (value_security v1).(indirect_reader) (security_of T).(indirect_reader) /\
  flows_to (value_security v2).(indirect_reader) (security_of T).(indirect_reader) /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ =>
      if is_low κ.(indirect_reader) then
        (exists k1 k2, v1 = tm_unit k1 /\ v2 = tm_unit k2)
      else True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      if is_low κ.(indirect_reader) then
        ((exists a1 a2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 k2 /\ value_rel T1 a1 a2) \/
         (exists a1 a2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inr a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 k2 /\ value_rel T2 a1 a2))
      else True
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      if is_low κ.(indirect_reader) then
        (exists a1 a2 b1 b2 k1 k2,
           v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\ value_rel T1 a1 a2 /\ value_rel T2 b1 b2)
      else True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      if is_low κ.(indirect_reader) then
        (forall (u1 u2 : tm) (r : label),
           value_rel T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ.(reader) r ->
           forall w1 w2 : tm,
             evaluates (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 -> evaluates (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 -> value_rel T2 w1 w2)
      else True
  end.

Definition term_rel (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2 : tm, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> value_rel T v1 v2.

Lemma term_rel_of_value_rel : forall T v1 v2, value_rel T v1 v2 -> term_rel T v1 v2.

Lemma multi_pair_inv : forall t w, multi t w -> value w ->

  - subst t. inversion Hw. (* value (tm_pair a b κ) — inversion gives value a, value b, but w = t? In the refl case, the second index is t, so w = t = pair a b κ. Lemma multi_app_inv : forall a b r w, multi (tm_app a b r) w -> value w -> exists ...
Proof.
  intros a b r w H.
  remember (tm_app a b r) as t eqn:E.
  revert a b r E.

  - subst; inversion Hw.  (* Hw : value w, but w = t? *)

Then remember; revert a b r E; now the goal is `forall a b r, t = app a b r -> value w -> exists ...` where t and w are the indices of H. Then `induction H` with the motive `fun t0 w0 => forall a b r, t0 = app a b r -> value w0 -> exists (with w0 as the ending value)`. Coq should generalize w0 automatically since it's an index of H and appears in the goal ✓. So induction H gives:
- refl case: t0 = w0 = t (the same variable). Goal: forall a b r, t = app a b r -> value t -> exists with multi ... ending at t.
- step case: t0 = x, w0 = w, hypothesis step x y and multi y w; IH: forall a b r, y = app a b r -> value w -> exists...; goal: forall a b r, x = app a b r -> value w -> exists....

In the step case: E : x = app a b r; subst x; Hs : step (app a b r) y; invert Hs.

Let me now write the environment machinery:

Definition subst_env (ρ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => ρ x
  | tm_unit κ => tm_unit κ
  | tm_abs T body κ => tm_abs T (subst_env ρ body) κ
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_env ρ t1) (subst_env ρ t2) r
  | tm_pair t1 t2 κ => tm_pair (subst_env ρ t1) (subst_env ρ t2) κ
  | tm_fst t1 r => tm_fst (subst_env ρ t1) r
  | tm_snd t1 r => tm_snd (subst_env ρ t1) r
  | tm_inl t1 κ => tm_inl (subst_env ρ t1) κ
  | tm_inr t1 κ => tm_inr (subst_env ρ t1) κ
  | tm_case t0 b1 b2 r => tm_case (subst_env ρ t0) (subst_env ρ b1) (subst_env ρ b2) r
  | tm_protect l t1 => tm_protect l (subst_env ρ t1)
  | tm_if c y n r => tm_if (subst_env ρ c) (subst_env ρ y) (subst_env ρ n) r
  end.

Wait, should subst_env go under binders? Yes: substituting free variables must go under binders (fvars are global); bvars untouched. So abs body is substituted ✓ (as in the given subst).

Definition extend_env (ρ : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then u else ρ y.

Hmm, which direction for eqb: `Nat.eqb y x` true iff y = x. Fine.

Definition range_lc (ρ : atom -> tm) : Prop := forall y, locally_closed (ρ y).

Definition env_rel (Γ : context) (ρ1 ρ2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  range_lc ρ1 /\ range_lc ρ2 /\
  forall x T, lookup_context x Γ = Some T -> term_rel T (ρ1 x) (ρ2 x).

Theorem fundamental : forall Γ t T, has_type Γ t T ->

Lemma multi_app_inv : forall a b r w,
  multi (tm_app a b r) w -> value w ->

Actually `intros rho x u body k Hr Hx` before induction? Let me look at the current proof:

```
Lemma subst_env_open_rec : forall rho x u body k,
  range_lc rho -> ~ In x (fv body) ->
  ...
Proof.
  induction body as [...];
  intros rho x u k Hr Hx; simpl in *.
```
Before the induction, the goal is `forall rho x u body k, ...`. `induction body` — body is not introduced yet (it's bound in the goal), so Coq will auto-intro the preceding binders rho x u (naming them rho, x, u), then induct on body, then the remaining intros... The error "rho is already used" occurs because after induction, the context has rho (auto-introduced) and then my `intros rho x u k Hr Hx` tries to introduce another rho.

Fix: `intros rho0 x u body k Hr Hx`? But then the induction must be after introducing body. Better structure:
```
Proof.
  intros rho x u body k Hr Hx.
  revert rho x u k Hr Hx.
  induction body as [...]; intros rho x u k Hr Hx; simpl in *.
```
Hmm, `revert` then induction then re-intro with the same names: after revert, the context has no rho; the induction's auto-context may introduce variables named rho? No: after revert, the goal is `forall rho x u k, range_lc rho -> ... -> subst_env ... = ...`; induction on body generalizes body and keeps the rest as binders in the goal (not introduced). So `induction body` with body a variable in the context? Wait, body is a variable of the context after intros; induction on it will revert the goal's dependencies: the goal mentions body, so induction generalizes. The remaining binders rho x u k stay in the goal as products (since they were reintroduced by revert). Hmm, after `revert rho x u k Hr Hx`, the context no longer has them, and the goal is `forall rho x u k, ...`. Then `induction body` where body : tm in the context and the goal is a product — Coq's induction on body with the goal as a product: it will generalize the conclusion over the products; then in each case the goal is the product `forall rho x u k, ...`. Then `intros rho x u k Hr Hx` works, provided the induction's context doesn't already name something rho. Since rho was reverted, fine.

So change to:
```
Proof.
  intros rho x u body k Hr Hx.
  revert rho x u k Hr Hx.
  induction body as [...]; intros rho x u k Hr Hx; simpl in *.
```
Hmm, but `revert rho x u k Hr Hx` before induction: `revert` with a list works.

Wait, actually the simpler standard pattern: `induction body as [...]; intros rho x u k Hr Hx.` — the problem is that Coq auto-introduced rho x u *before* the induction (to have body as a variable). So we can't re-intro rho. The revert approach is right.

Similarly for other lemmas I used the pattern `induction t as [...]; intros k k' Hle H.` where the statement is `forall t k k', ...` — here t comes first, so no auto-intro of k k' happens ✓ (t is the first binder). For lc_open_rec_fvar: statement `forall body x k, ...` — body first ✓. For subst_env_open_rec: statement `forall rho x u body k, ...` — body is 4th, so rho x u get auto-introduced ✗. So define context_fv:
Fixpoint context_fv (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs T C' k => context_fv C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t r => fv t ++ context_fv C'
  | C_AppRight t C' r => fv t ++ context_fv C'
  | C_PairLeft C' t k => fv t ++ context_fv C'
  | C_PairRight t C' k => fv t ++ context_fv C'
  | C_Fst C' r => context_fv C'
  | C_Snd C' r => context_fv C'
  | C_Inl C' k => context_fv C'
  | C_Inr C' k => context_fv C'
  | C_CaseScrutinee C' b1 b2 r => context_fv C' ++ fv b1 ++ fv b2
  | C_CaseLeft t0 C' b2 r => fv t0 ++ context_fv C' ++ fv b2
  | C_CaseRight t0 b1 C' r => fv t0 ++ fv b1 ++ context_fv C'
  | C_Protect l C' => context_fv C'
  | C_IfCondition C' y n r => context_fv C' ++ fv y ++ fv n
  | C_IfThen c C' n r => fv c ++ context_fv C' ++ fv n
  | C_IfElse c y C' r => fv c ++ fv y ++ context_fv C'
  end.

Lemma fv_plug : forall C u, fv (plug C u) = fv u ++ context_fv C.
Proof by induction on C; simpl; rewrite IH; repeat rewrite app_assoc; reflexivity. The list concatenation ordering needs care but should be provable with `simpl; rewrite IHC; repeat rewrite <- app_assoc; reflexivity` or list lemmas. Let me think: For C_AppLeft C' t r: plug = app (plug C' u) t r; fv = fv (plug C' u) ++ fv t = (fv u ++ context_fv C') ++ fv t. RHS = fv u ++ (fv t ++ context_fv C'). These are equal up to associativity ✓.
For C_CaseScrutinee C' b1 b2 r: plug = case (plug C' u) b1 b2 r; fv = fv (plug C' u) ++ fv b1 ++ fv b2 = ((fv u ++ cfv C') ++ fv b1) ++ fv b2. RHS = fv u ++ ((cfv C' ++ fv b1) ++ fv b2) ✓ assoc.
So `induction C; simpl; try reflexivity; try (rewrite IHC; repeat rewrite app_assoc; reflexivity)` etc. Let me handle with a generic script: `induction C; simpl; try reflexivity; try (rewrite IHC; repeat rewrite app_assoc; reflexivity); try (rewrite IHC1, IHC2; ...)`. Since each constructor has at most one context, we can use IHC. Then `f_equal`? Let me just do `rewrite IHC; repeat rewrite <- app_assoc; reflexivity` maybe. The associativity orientation may vary; `repeat rewrite app_assoc` normalizes to right-assoc? `app_assoc : forall l1 l2 l3, (l1 ++ l2) ++ l3 = l1 ++ (l2 ++ l3)`. `repeat rewrite app_assoc` rewrites left-assoc into right-assoc, giving a canonical form; both sides normalize to the same right-assoc form ✓. But the goal has an equality of lists with possibly different nesting; repeat rewrite app_assoc on the goal rewrites both sides to right-assoc; then reflexivity ✓.

Lemma has_type_fv : forall Γ t T, has_type Γ t T -> forall y, In y (fv t) -> exists U, lookup_context y Γ = Some U.

Define:
Fixpoint context_closed (C : program_context) : Prop :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => True
  | C_Abs T C' k => context_closed C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t r => context_closed C' /\ fv t = []
  | C_AppRight t C' r => fv t = [] /\ context_closed C'
  | C_PairLeft C' t k => context_closed C' /\ fv t = []
  | C_PairRight t C' k => fv t = [] /\ context_closed C'
  | C_Fst C' r | C_Snd C' r | C_Inl C' k | C_Inr C' k | C_Protect l C' => context_closed C'
  | C_CaseScrutinee C' b1 b2 r => context_closed C' /\ fv b1 = [] /\ fv b2 = []
  | C_CaseLeft t0 C' b2 r => fv t0 = [] /\ context_closed C' /\ fv b2 = []
  | C_CaseRight t0 b1 C' r => fv t0 = [] /\ fv b1 = [] /\ context_closed C'
  | C_IfCondition C' y n r => context_closed C' /\ fv y = [] /\ fv n = []
  | C_IfThen c C' n r => fv c = [] /\ context_closed C' /\ fv n = []
  | C_IfElse c y C' r => fv c = [] /\ fv y = [] /\ context_closed C'
  end.

Definition env_rel (Gamma : context) (rho1 rho2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  range_lc rho1 /\ range_lc rho2 /\
  forall x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> term_rel T (rho1 x) (rho2 x).

Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall rho1 rho2, env_rel Gamma rho1 rho2 ->
  term_rel T (subst_env rho1 t) (subst_env rho2 t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H; intros rho1 rho2 Henv.

Lemma value_rel_sum_inl : forall T1 T2 kappa a1 a2 k1 k2,
  is_low kappa.ind = true ->
  value_rel (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa) (tm_inl a1 k1) (tm_inl a2 k2) ->
  value_rel T1 a1 a2.
Proof.

Let me define value_rel_sum_inv:
```coq
Lemma value_rel_sum_inv : forall T1 T2 kappa v1 v2,
  is_low kappa.(indirect_reader) = true ->
  value_rel (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa) v1 v2 ->
  ((exists (a1 a2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
      v1 = tm_inl a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 k2 /\ value_rel T1 a1 a2) \/
   (exists (a1 a2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
      v1 = tm_inr a1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 k2 /\ value_rel T2 a1 a2)).
Proof.
  intros T1 T2 kappa v1 v2 Hlow Hrel.
  simpl in Hrel. rewrite Hlow in Hrel. simpl in Hrel.
  destruct Hrel as [_ [_ [_ [_ Hst]]]]. exact Hst.
Qed.
```
Hmm: `simpl in Hrel` — Hrel : value_rel (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa) v1 v2. simpl with the constructor type reduces the Fixpoint ✓ but the recursive value_rel calls on T1/T2 are variables so they stay. Lemma value_rel_sum_inl_inr : forall T1 T2 kappa (a1 a2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),

value_rel_prod_inv:
```coq
Lemma value_rel_prod_inv : forall T1 T2 kappa v1 v2,
  is_low kappa.(indirect_reader) = true ->
  value_rel (Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa) v1 v2 ->
  exists (a1 a2 b1 b2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
    v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\
    value_rel T1 a1 a2 /\ value_rel T2 b1 b2.
Proof.
  intros. simpl in H0. rewrite H in H0. simpl in H0.
  destruct H0 as [_ [_ [_ [_ Hst]]]]. exact Hst.
Qed.
```

value_rel_arrow_inv:
```coq
Lemma value_rel_arrow_inv : forall T1 T2 kappa v1 v2,
  is_low kappa.(indirect_reader) = true ->
  value_rel (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) v1 v2 ->
  forall u1 u2 r, value_rel T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to kappa.(reader) r ->
    forall w1 w2, evaluates (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 -> evaluates (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 ->
    value_rel T2 w1 w2.
Proof.
  intros. simpl in H0. rewrite H in H0. simpl in H0.

Qed.
```

evaluates_protect_low:
```coq
Lemma evaluates_protect_low : forall k t w,
  k.(indirect_reader) = Low ->
  multi (tm_protect k.(indirect_reader) t) w -> value w -> evaluates t w.
Proof.
  intros k t w Hk Hm Hw. rewrite Hk in Hm.
  edestruct (multi_protect_inv Low t w Hm Hw) as (u & Htu & Hu & Ew).
  rewrite protect_value_low in Ew by exact Hu. subst w.
  split; assumption.
Qed.
```
Note: multi_protect_inv's first arg is l = Low ✓.

Qed.
```

Lemma env_rel_extend : forall Gamma rho1 rho2 x T v1 v2,
  env_rel Gamma rho1 rho2 ->
  value v1 -> value v2 -> value_rel T v1 v2 ->
  env_rel (update Gamma x T) (extend_env rho1 x v1) (extend_env rho2 x v2).
Proof.
  intros Gamma rho1 rho2 x T v1 v2 [Hr1 [Hr2 Hlk]] Hv1 Hv2 Hrel.
  unfold env_rel. repeat split.
  - apply range_lc_extend; [exact Hr1 | apply value_lc; exact Hv1].

  - intros y U Hlu. destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:Eyx.

      rewrite lookup_update_same in Hlu. injection Hlu as <-.

      rewrite lookup_update_neq in Hlu by exact Hyx.
      rewrite extend_env_neq by exact Hyx.
      rewrite extend_env_neq by exact Hyx.
      apply Hlk; exact Hlu.
Qed.

```coq
Theorem noninterference : forall (C : program_context) (t1 t2 : tm)
    (T_secret T_result : ty),
  has_type empty t1 T_secret ->
  has_type empty t2 T_secret ->
  context_has_type C T_secret T_result ->
  ground T_result ->
  transparent T_result ->
  ~ flows_to (security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader)
      (security_of T_result).(indirect_reader) ->
  same_result (plug C t1) (plug C t2).
Proof.
  intros C t1 t2 T_secret T_result Ht1 Ht2 [L HC] Hground Htrans Hnflow.
  (* derive label facts *)

    - reflexivity.
    - exfalso. apply Hnflow. destruct ((security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader)); simpl; exact I. }
  assert (Hsec : (security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader) = High).
  { destruct ((security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader)) eqn:E.
    - exfalso. apply Hnflow. destruct ((security_of T_result).(indirect_reader)); simpl; exact I.
    - reflexivity. }
